26/12/2025 01:42		
		
Good question. This is the right challenge.		
I’ll answer it cleanly, without defensiveness, without selling, and by contrasting with existing HR systems—because if this can’t beat them conceptually, the product shouldn’t exist.		
		
1️⃣ “Just by naming will it be borderless?” — No. Name is irrelevant		
		
You’re right:		
Calling something BorderOps (or anything) does not make it borderless.		
		
Borderless is not a label, it’s a property of the system.		
		
So let’s remove the name entirely for this explanation.		
		
2️⃣ “Why not just use existing HR platforms?” — THIS is the core question		
		
You are right again:		
		
HR is one of the oldest enterprise system categories		
		
There are hundreds of HR ERP / HRIS / ATS tools		
		
If the pain still exists, either:		
		
Everyone is incompetent (unlikely), or		
		
The pain is structural, not implementation-level		
		
Let’s examine this precisely.		
		
3️⃣ What HR systems are ACTUALLY built for (fact)		
		
Traditional HR systems evolved to solve stable, internal problems:		
		
What HR systems optimize for		
		
Employee records		
		
Payroll within a country		
		
Leave, attendance		
		
Performance reviews		
		
Compliance inside one jurisdiction		
		
Headcount reporting		
		
They assume:		
		
One employer		
		
One country (or a few)		
		
One legal framework		
		
Long-term employment		
		
This assumption is baked into their data models.		
		
4️⃣ What changed in the real world (this is where the gap appears)		
The modern reality (post-2015, accelerated post-2020)		
		
Work is:		
		
Cross-border		
		
Contract-based		
		
Platform-mediated		
		
Remote-first		
		
One person can be:		
		
Contractor in country A		
		
Employee in country B		
		
Freelancer on 2 platforms		
		
Companies hire:		
		
Before entity setup		
		
Via EOR		
		
Via payroll partners		
		
Via intermediaries		
		
This breaks HR assumptions.		
		
5️⃣ Why the pain STILL exists despite many HR tools		
		
Because no HR system owns the full problem.		
		
Let’s be explicit.		
		
HR tools are split into silos		
System	Owns	Does NOT own
ATS	Hiring pipeline	Legal feasibility
HRIS	Employee records	Pre-hire readiness
Payroll	Salary & tax	Hiring decision logic
EOR	Compliance execution	Talent evaluation
ITSM	Access provisioning	Legal/HR dependency logic
		
Each tool works locally, not globally.		
		
6️⃣ The REAL pain point (now stated precisely)		
		
No system answers this question end-to-end:		
“Can this person legally, operationally, and productively work for us in this role, in this country, starting on this date — and why?”		
		
HR systems answer partial questions:		
		
“Is the employee in our system?”		
		
“Is payroll configured?”		
		
“Is access requested?”		
		
They do not answer:		
		
“What will block this before it breaks?”		
		
“What dependencies exist across teams?”		
		
“What happens when any variable changes?”		
		
That’s why spreadsheets + emails still exist.		
		
7️⃣ Why HR being “old” actually makes the gap worse		
		
Older systems are:		
		
Schema-heavy		
		
Rule-based		
		
Deterministic		
		
Built for predictability		
		
Cross-border work is:		
		
Contextual		
		
Exception-driven		
		
Policy-shifting		
		
Ambiguous		
		
This mismatch is not fixable by adding features to HRIS.		
		
8️⃣ So what is fundamentally DIFFERENT about this system?		
Key distinction (this matters)		
		
HR systems = record-keeping systems		
This system = reasoning system		
		
HR systems store:		
		
“What is true right now”		
		
This system reasons:		
		
“What will become a problem if we proceed”		
		
“What depends on what”		
		
“What breaks if X changes”		
		
That requires AI.		
		
9️⃣ Why AI is mandatory here (not optional)		
		
HR rules explode combinatorially when:		
		
Country × role × contract × policy × platform × time		
		
Humans manage this with:		
		
Experience		
		
Gut feeling		
		
Escalation		
		
AI manages this with:		
		
Dependency reasoning		
		
Document interpretation		
		
Conflict detection		
		
Scenario simulation		
		
This is not CRUD + chatbot.		
		
🔟 Why this pain persists EVEN in “good companies”		
		
Because:		
		
The cost is distributed (HR, legal, IT each feel a piece)		
		
No single team owns “global operational readiness”		
		
Failures show up as:		
		
Delays		
		
Frustration		
		
Silent inefficiency		
(not a single red error)		
		
Those are the hardest problems to kill.		
		
11️⃣ Borderless ≠ replaces HR		
		
Important correction:		
		
This system is not an HR replacement.		
		
It sits:		
		
Across HR, Legal, IT, Payroll		
		
Before, during, and after hire		
		
As a coordination & reasoning layer		
		
Think of it as:		
		
“The system that explains why HR systems are stuck.”		
12️⃣ Why THIS can exist alongside HR platforms		
		
Because:		
		
HR platforms don’t want to own cross-border reasoning risk		
		
EOR vendors don’t want to own hiring logic		
		
Payroll vendors don’t want to own document truth		
		
IT doesn’t want to own legal dependencies		
		
This gap is intentional — but painful.		
		
13️⃣ Final clarity (no selling)		
		
❌ It’s not borderless because of the name		
		
❌ It’s not better because HR tools are “bad”		
		
✅ It exists because no system reasons across borders and silos		
		
✅ AI is required because rules are contextual and dynamic		
		
✅ The pain persists because responsibility is fragmented		
One final grounding question (not rhetorical)		
		
If tomorrow:		
		
HR tools disappeared,		
		
Payroll tools disappeared,		
		
EOR tools disappeared,		
		
Would a human still struggle to coordinate global hiring?		
Yes — because the struggle is reasoning, not storage.		
		
That’s the problem this system exists to solve.		
		
If you want, next we can:		
		
Pick one concrete scenario (e.g., hire contractor in X → employee in Y)		
		
Walk it step by step		
		
Show exactly where HR tools stop and this system starts		
		
No design. Just reality.		







26/12/2025 01:47

Below is one concrete scenario and a step-by-step walk showing exactly where HR tools stop and where this system starts. I’ll keep it operational: who does what, what tools exist today, what breaks, and what AI does.

I’ll use a realistic pattern (no country-specific legal claims):
Hire as contractor in Country X → convert to employee in Country Y after 3 months.

Scenario setup

Company: UK-based remote company
Role: Senior Data Engineer
Candidate: Lives in Country X now, plans to relocate to Country Y in ~3 months
Goal: Start work quickly as contractor in X, then convert to employee in Y with minimal disruption.

Tools the company already has (typical):

ATS (e.g., Greenhouse/Lever) for hiring pipeline

HRIS (e.g., Workday/BambooHR) for employee records

Payroll/EOR vendor (e.g., Deel/Remote/Oyster) for cross-border employment/contracting

IT ticketing (Jira/ServiceNow) for access/equipment

Shared docs (Google Drive/Notion) for policies/templates
Step-by-step: where HR tools stop vs where the system starts
Step 1 — Requisition & plan (before job ad)
What HR tools do today

ATS stores the job requisition.

HR may add “location: remote” and a note: “contractor first, then employee”.

✅ HR tools can store the plan.
❌ They do not validate whether the plan is operationally coherent.

Where the system starts

System action (AI reasoning): “Plan feasibility + dependency map”

Inputs: role, start date target, current country X, future country Y, engagement plan (contractor→employee), internal policy pack (security level, equipment, data access constraints), preferred vendor (EOR).

Output:

Two-track operational plan (contractor track now; employee track later)

Dependency graph with dates (what must be done before what)

Known friction points (e.g., identity consistency, contract/role continuity, access and payroll changeover)

Evidence checklist (what to collect once vs what differs by track)

Why HR tools stop here: ATS/HRIS don’t reason across payroll+IT+legal+time.

Step 2 — CV screening and interviews
What HR tools do today

ATS manages resumes, interview stages, scorecards.

Hiring manager collects feedback.

✅ This part is ATS-native.

Where the system starts (optional but powerful)

System action (AI): “Claim consistency + evidence map”

Reads CV + LinkedIn + portfolio + interview notes.

Builds an “evidence map”: claimed skills ↔ proof artifacts.

Flags likely future issues: ambiguous dates, overlapping employment, unclear client work (common contractor risk).

Why it matters for later: Contractor→employee conversions often trigger deeper checks; catching inconsistencies early prevents later churn.

Step 3 — Decision: “Hire them”
What HR tools do today

ATS marks “hired”.

HR sends offer / begins onboarding.

✅ ATS closes the loop.
❌ No tool answers: “What is the fastest safe route to Day-1 productivity given the two-track plan?”

Where the system starts

System action (AI): “Day-1 readiness plan”

Produces:

Minimum viable access set for contractor start

Equipment/shipping plan (if any)

Compliance tasks split by owner (HR/worker/vendor/IT)

Timeline with “hard blockers vs soft blockers”

This is the first place you see work starting as a computed outcome, not a hopeful date.

Step 4 — Contractor onboarding in Country X (start work)
What exists today

Vendor/EOR portal collects contractor agreement + bank info + tax form equivalents.

HRIS may not store contractors properly, or stores them in a separate module.

IT provisions accounts from a ticket request.

Documents are scattered (Drive + vendor portal + HR email).

✅ Each system does its own intake.
❌ Nobody maintains a single “truth state” of what’s done and what’s missing.

Where the system starts (core)

System action (AI): “Evidence ingestion + validation”

Worker uploads once (or forwards emails / connects sources).

AI extracts structured facts (name, address, dates, identifiers, contract terms), and runs cross-source consistency checks.

Produces a live status:

✅ Done

⚠ Needs review

⛔ Blocking

System action (AI): “Exception killer”

Detects issues that currently cause human ping-pong:

Name spelling mismatch across documents

Start date mismatch (offer vs vendor agreement)

Contract type mismatch (contractor vs employee wording)

Missing mandatory fields for vendor submission

Suggests exact fix and who must do it.

Where HR tools stop: they record what you upload; they don’t reconcile and explain conflicts across systems.

Step 5 — Work begins (Week 1–12 as contractor)
What exists today

Timesheets in vendor portal or separate time tool.

HRIS record stale; IT access grows organically (“just grant them X”).

No one prepares for conversion; it’s remembered late.

✅ Work happens.
❌ The conversion is a future event that gets ignored until it’s urgent.

Where the system starts

System action (AI): “Conversion runway manager”

Starts a countdown from Day 1:

What must be collected continuously (proof of work, payroll history, performance artifacts, address changes)

What must be planned (employee contract, benefits, entity/EOR choice, access policy changes)

Creates scheduled tasks for HR and worker with deadlines tied to planned move date.

This is the “not one-off” part. It’s continuous.

Step 6 — Relocation trigger: “Moving to Country Y”
What exists today

Worker emails HR: “I’m moving on X date.”

HR restarts discovery: “What do we need in Country Y?”

Vendor proposes steps; IT re-checks security.

✅ People can do it manually.
❌ It’s slow, repetitive, and fragile.

Where the system starts (big)

System action (AI): “Scenario recomputation”

Recomputes the entire dependency graph with new location and employee status:

Employee onboarding requirements in Y

Contract changes needed

Payroll setup requirements

IT/security policy changes (e.g., data access constraints)

Outputs a conversion plan with:

Freeze dates (when contractor invoices stop)

Cutover date (employee start)

Parallel run plan (to avoid pay gaps / access loss)

Where HR tools stop: HRIS stores “location changed.” It does not compute the operational cutover.

Step 7 — Employee onboarding in Country Y
What exists today

HRIS employee onboarding tasks (forms, benefits).

Vendor collects employee documents.

Legal reviews contract.

IT changes access policies.

✅ Each tool covers its local tasks.
❌ The conversion still risks:

Pay gap

Duplicate onboarding

Conflicting records (contractor vs employee)

Access over/under-provisioning
Where the system starts

System action (AI): “Identity continuity + record merge”

Ensures the worker is one identity across:

Contractor record(s)

Employee record

Vendor profile

IT accounts

Flags anything that would cause duplicate records or compliance confusion.

System action (AI): “Cutover safety checks”

Before the cutover date, AI verifies:

All blocking requirements satisfied

Contract aligned with role reality

Payroll readiness confirmed

IT access adjustments scheduled

This is a “release checklist”, but computed from real evidence, not a static list.

Step 8 — After conversion: ongoing operations
What exists today

HRIS runs.

Payroll runs.

Vendor tickets happen.

Drift is discovered late (expired docs, policy changes).
Where the system starts

System action (AI): “Drift monitor”

Watches:

Document expiries

Policy pack updates

Role changes

Cross-border travel events (if relevant)

Alerts before it breaks payroll/access/compliance.
The boundary in one line

HR tools manage records and workflow inside their own silo.
This system manages the cross-silo reasoning: dependencies, conflicts, cutovers, and continuous readiness.

What you should notice (why this isn’t “another HR platform”)

In this scenario, the system’s value appears in 4 moments where HR tools cannot help:

Two-track planning (contractor now → employee later)

Conflict detection across documents + systems

Cutover computation (no pay/access gap)

Continuous runway + drift monitoring

That’s where human time is currently burned.

EXACT “Where HR tools stop / system starts” in this scenario (pinpoint)

HR tools handle:

ATS stages, interview scheduling, employee record storage, payroll execution inside vendor portals.

This system starts at:

Two-track plan computation (contractor now + employee later with dependencies)

Cross-document consistency (identity continuity)

Cutover planning + safety checks (no pay/access gap)

Drift monitoring (things changing over months)

Those are not “features” of HRIS; they’re cross-silo reasoning.

If you want this to be “anti-amnesia” for execution

Full list of AI calls (20–30 calls) with stable IDs (A1…A30)

Exact screen component list per screen (buttons, fields, validations)

Exact database entities needed to persist every artifact used above (plans, tasks, docs, states, audits)

Just say: “Continue: full AI call catalog + DB entity list.”

3) DB ENTITY LIST (tables + key fields)

This is the minimum full-fledged schema to persist every artifact above. PostgreSQL + pgvector assumed.

Core org/auth
orgs

id (PK), name, created_at
users

id, org_id (FK), email, name, role (HR|HM|IT|FIN|WORKER|ADMIN), created_at
memberships

user_id, org_id, role, status
Planning & roles
hiring_plans

id, org_id, title, initial_country, future_country, strategy_json, start_by, status, created_by, created_at
role_profiles

id, org_id, plan_id, title, seniority, security_level, stack_json, constraints_json
rubrics

id, org_id, plan_id, version, rubric_json, created_at
interview_kits

id, org_id, rubric_id, version, kit_json
Candidates & sources
candidates

id, org_id, plan_id, name, email, stage, created_at
candidate_sources

id, candidate_id, source_type (cv_pdf|linkedin|portfolio|github|work_sample), source_value, fetched_artifact_id, status
artifacts

id, org_id, type (html_snapshot|pdf|text|image), storage_uri, sha256, created_at
candidate_profiles

id, candidate_id, version, profile_json, confidence, created_at
evidence_maps

id, candidate_id, version, evidence_map_json, created_at
Interviews
interviews

id, candidate_id, round, scheduled_at, interviewer_id, status
interview_notes

id, interview_id, raw_notes_text, structured_json, confidence, created_at
contradictions

id, candidate_id, contradiction_json, severity, created_at
decisions

id, candidate_id, decision (hire|no_hire|hold), rationale_json, created_at
Offers, contracts, documents
offers

id, candidate_id, engagement_type, country, terms_json, status, created_at
contracts

id, offer_id, contract_type, template_id, filled_fields_json, review_flags_json, status
documents

id, org_id, owner_type (candidate|worker|org), owner_id, doc_type, artifact_id, extracted_fields_json, validations_json, confidence, created_at
doc_conflicts

id, owner_type, owner_id, conflicts_json, blocking, created_at
Workers & onboarding
workers

id, org_id, candidate_id (nullable), name, email, current_country, status, created_at
engagements

id, worker_id, phase (contractor|employee), country, start_date, end_date, vendor_ref, status
tasks

id, org_id, subject_type (candidate|worker|plan), subject_id, phase, owner_role, owner_user_id (nullable),
name, description, blocking, depends_on_json, status, due_date, created_at
task_events

id, task_id, event_type, payload_json, created_at
Access / IT
access_requests

id, worker_id, requested_access_json, approved_access_json, controls_json, status, created_at
it_tickets

id, worker_id, external_ref, status, created_at
Timesheets & pay
timesheets

id, worker_id, period, hours_json, status, ai_check_json, created_at
payments

id, worker_id, period, amount, currency, status, reconciliation_json, created_at
Cutover / drift
cutovers

id, worker_id, from_phase, to_phase, plan_json, status, created_at
drift_alerts

id, org_id, subject_type, subject_id, severity, message, action, refs_json, status, created_at
Policy & rules
policy_packs

id, org_id, policy_type, version_label, artifact_id, extracted_sections_json, created_at
country_rules

id, org_id, country, version, rules_json, source_refs_json, created_at
AI ops
ai_runs

id, org_id, call_id (A1..A32), subject_type, subject_id, input_json, output_json,
model_name, tokens_in, tokens_out, latency_ms, created_at
prompt_templates

id, org_id, prompt_id, template_text, expected_schema_json, status, created_at

You’re right: my Scenario-2 write-up still had compressed bits (“for brevity”, “phase 2”) and some steps were described, not walked.

Now I will rewrite Scenario-2 as a strict, step-by-step operating manual with:

What “Phase 2” means (no ambiguity)

Every step in order, with which screen, exact clicks, what the user sees

Exact AI call per step with full example input + full example output (not schemas)

No cutting. No “same as before”. No placeholders like “xxx”.

Scenario 2 (FULL DETAIL)

Employee in Country Y from Day-1, but candidate is in Country X; remote start from X for 30 days; then relocation to Y.

What “Phase 2” means (clear definition)

Phase 1 = Remote-Start Phase (Work from X, employee contract in Y, restricted access + provisional payroll readiness).
Phase 2 = Local-Work Phase (After relocation to Y: address/tax/payroll finalization + access policy switch + steady-state).

So “Phase 2” is not “contractor → employee”.
It is employee throughout, but the operational conditions change after relocation.

Global objects (IDs used consistently)

ORG_ID = ORG123

PLAN_ID = PLAN_EMP_Y_REMOTE_X_001

ROLE_ID = ROLE_SDE_001

CAND_ID = CAND002

WORKER_ID = WORK001 (candidate becomes worker after hire)

Countries: X (current) → Y (employment country + future work location)

Remote period: 30 days

Dates (example):

Offer accepted: 2026-01-05

Start date (remote): 2026-01-20

Planned relocation date: 2026-02-19

Local phase begins: 2026-02-20

DB		
		
doc_conflicts INSERT

TASK STATE PROPAGATION (IMPORTANT)		
		
If any blocking rule fails, system does:		
		
1) Insert doc_conflicts		
2) Update tasks.status = blocked		
3) Create remediation task		
		
Example:		
		
{		
"task_id":"T_FIX_ADDR",		
"owner_role":"Worker",		
"name":"Upload valid address proof (issued within 90 days)",		
"blocking":true		
}

This guarantees:		
		
No hallucinated “validation”		
		
Deterministic behaviour		
		
Auditable decision trail		
		
Zero “AI magic” claims

Field-level extraction

Row-level persistence

26/12/2025 02:24

Below is a FULL-FLEDGED, NON-SUMMARISED, NO-SHRINK, NO-SAFE-MODE PROJECT STATEMENT written explicitly for future pages so that if I drift, forget, or try to simplify, this document becomes the source of truth.

This is not marketing, not pitch, not abstraction.
This is what the system IS, what it DOES, and what scenarios it MUST support.

You can copy-paste this into a new page and treat it as canonical memory.

PROJECT CANONICAL DEFINITION

(ANTI-DRIFT / ANTI-AMNESIA BASELINE)

Project Nature (what this is)

This project is a cross-border work orchestration and reasoning system.

It is NOT:

an HRIS

an ATS

a payroll system

an EOR

a document storage app

a chatbot

It does not replace those systems.

It exists because those systems do not reason together.

The Core Problem (precise, not generic)

Modern work is borderless, multi-phase, and event-driven, but enterprise systems are:

country-locked

phase-blind

siloed

record-keeping oriented

As a result, human coordination cost explodes whenever:

work starts in one country and continues in another

engagement type changes (contractor ↔ employee)

location changes mid-employment

access, payroll, and compliance must change without disruption

Today, humans solve this with:

spreadsheets

emails

Slack escalations

tribal knowledge

This system exists to replace that coordination burden with deterministic reasoning.

What the System Actually Does (operationally)

The system:

Models work as phases, not static states

Models dependencies across HR, Legal, IT, Finance

Ingests evidence once (CVs, IDs, addresses, tax forms, contracts)

Validates evidence deterministically

Creates task graphs instead of checklists

Recomputes requirements when reality changes

Prevents pay gaps, access gaps, and compliance breaks

Produces auditable reasoning trails

This is a reasoning + orchestration layer, not a CRUD system.

The Core Design Rule (NON-NEGOTIABLE)

Nothing is assumed stable.
Country, role, access, engagement, payroll, and policy can all change.

Therefore:

Every step must be stateful

Every state change must trigger re-evaluation

Every decision must be explainable from stored evidence
System Boundaries (clear)
The system STARTS where:

sequencing

cutovers

Plans (intended future states)

Decisions (with justification)

SCENARIO GROUP C — FAILURE / EXCEPTION HANDLING

Fragmented history

D3. Policy update applies to only some workers

Selective remediation required

Evidence

Rules

Decisions

Dates

Produce traceable reasoning

Not rely on human memory

II. FAILURE-CASE WALKTHROUGHS (DETAILED, NOT ABSTRACT)

Below are three failure scenarios that MUST be supported explicitly, not implicitly.

FAILURE CASE F-1
Expired ID discovered after remote start but before payroll
Situation

Worker started remote work (Phase-1)

ID was valid at upload

ID expires before first payroll run

Payroll rules require valid ID at pay-run time
What typically happens today

Payroll rejects late

HR scrambles

Worker is unpaid or delayed

No single system shows root cause
REQUIRED SYSTEM BEHAVIOUR
Step 1 — Detection

A26 Drift Monitor runs nightly

Sees government_id.expiry_date < payroll_cutoff_date

Generates blocking alert
{
"alert_type": "DOCUMENT_EXPIRY",
"doc_id": "DOC_ID_001",
"severity": "high",
"blocking": true,
"impact": "Payroll cannot proceed",
"affected_phase": "phase_1_remote_start"
}
Step 2 — Task propagation

System must:

Block payroll task

Create remediation task
{
"task_id": "T_REUPLOAD_ID",
"owner_role": "Worker",
"name": "Upload renewed government ID",
"blocking": true,
"due_before": "2026-01-28"
}
Step 3 — Communication

Worker notified (Screen 16)

HR notified (Dashboard alert)

Finance notified (Pay Hub warning)
Step 4 — Resolution

Worker uploads new ID

A17 validates

A18 consistency check passes

Payroll task unblocks automatically

No human coordination required.

FAILURE CASE F-2
Relocation delayed by 2 weeks (Phase-2 slips)
Situation

Planned relocation: Feb 19

Actual relocation: Mar 5

Phase-2 tasks were scheduled based on old date
What typically happens today

Tasks fire too early

Payroll setup wrong

Access policies misaligned
REQUIRED SYSTEM BEHAVIOUR
Step 1 — Event input

Worker updates status:

{
"event": {
"type": "relocation_delay",
"new_expected_date": "2026-03-05"
}
}
Step 2 — Scenario recompute

A24 — scenario_recompute_agent must:

Shift all phase-2 tasks forward

Recalculate payroll cutoff

Extend Phase-1 access rules

Re-evaluate rule compliance
{
"changes": [
{"task":"T6","old_due":"2026-02-19","new_due":"2026-03-05"},
{"task":"T8","old_due":"2026-02-25","new_due":"2026-03-11"}
],
"extended_phase": "phase_1_remote_start",
"new_phase_2_start": "2026-03-06"
}
Step 3 — Safety checks

System must verify:

Remote work still allowed under policy

No rule violation caused by extension

If extension exceeds policy:

Generate policy exception task (human review)
FAILURE CASE F-3
Name mismatch blocks tax form acceptance
Situation

ID: RAJESH KUMAR S

Tax form: Rajesh Kumar

Vendor rejects silently
REQUIRED SYSTEM BEHAVIOUR
Step 1 — Detection

A18 Cross-doc consistency flags mismatch

{
"conflict_type": "NAME_MISMATCH",
"canonical": "RAJESH KUMAR S",
"variant": "Rajesh Kumar",
"severity": "blocking"
}
Step 2 — Canonical resolution

System proposes fix:

Canonical name = ID name

Generate name-alignment instruction
Step 3 — Remediation task
{
"task_id": "T_FIX_TAX_NAME",
"owner_role": "Worker",
"name": "Re-submit tax form using canonical name",
"blocking": true
}
Step 4 — Audit

Decision + resolution stored for future audits.

# 1️⃣ CORE USE CASE (PRIMARY)

### UC-01 — Can this person WORK, right now and later?

**Actor:** HR / Ops / IT

**Question the system must answer:**

> “Is this person allowed to work in this role, in this country, on this date —  
and what EXACTLY blocks it if not?”

## Functional Requirements (Hard)

FR-01

- Role
- Engagement type (employee / contractor)
- Start date
- Current location
- Planned future location (optional)

FR-02

- ID
- Address proof(s)
- Contract(s)
- Tax forms
- Any future document type

FR-03  
System must **reason**, not store:
- Dependencies
- Blocking vs non-blocking
- Phase validity (remote / local / converted)
- Time-bound rules

FR-04  
On **any change** (date, country, role, doc):
- System must recompute readiness
- System must NOT assume past validity

FR-05  
System must output:
- READY / NOT READY
- Exact blockers
- Exact next action
- Owner (HR / Worker / IT / Finance)

NFR-01 Deterministic (no vibes)

## A. FRONTEND (WHAT USER SEES)

   - Date change

## B. BACKEND (CORE LOGIC)

### Tech
- **Python FastAPI**
- **Pydantic** for contracts

### Core Services (REAL CODE UNITS)

```
services/
 ├─ readiness_engine.py
 ├─ rule_engine.py
 ├─ phase_engine.py
 ├─ dependency_engine.py
 ├─ event_recompute.py
 ├─ evidence_validator.py
```

---

### readiness_engine.py
**Responsibility**
- Single entry point
- Answers: READY / NOT READY

**Input**
```json
{
  "person_id": "P123",
  "role": "Senior Data Engineer",
  "engagement": "employee",
  "country": "Y",
  "work_location": "X",
  "start_date": "2026-01-20"
}
```

**Output**
```json
{
  "status": "NOT_READY",
  "blockers": [
    {
      "type": "DOCUMENT",
      "name": "Address Proof (Y)",
      "owner": "Worker",
      "reason": "Required before payroll"
    }
  ]
}
```

---

### phase_engine.py
Determines:
- Phase = remote / local / converted
- What rules apply in this phase

---

### rule_engine.py
Pure logic:
- IF conditions
- No AI guesses
- Versioned rules

---

### dependency_engine.py
Builds:
- Task graph
- Blocking order

---

### event_recompute.py
Triggered on:
- Country change
- Date change
- Role change
- Doc update

Re-runs **entire readiness check**.

---

persons
documents
document_validations
rules
phases
tasks
events
readiness_snapshots
audit_logs

## 0) What this product MUST cover (no shrink)

BorderOps Runtime covers **end-to-end cross-border work operations**, not just “docs & readiness”:

1) **Pre-hire planning** (can we hire in route X→Y?)  
2) **Sourcing & evidence** (CV/LinkedIn/portfolio ingestion + evidence mapping)  
3) **Interview loops** (structured notes + contradiction checks)  
4) **Offer & contract pack** (templates + clause selection + approvals)  
5) **Onboarding orchestration** (task dependency graph, owners, deadlines)  
6) **Identity/evidence validation** (ID/address/tax/bank + deterministic rules)  
7) **IT access readiness** (policy-driven minimum access + phase-based escalation)  
8) **Work tracking** (tasks + work logs + timesheets)  
9) **Payroll/payments** (pay periods, approvals, exports, reconciliation, pay status)  
10) **Change events** (relocation/date slip/role change/vendor lead-time change)  
11) **Continuous drift monitoring** (expiries, policy changes, rule updates)  
12) **Audit & traceability** (every decision reproducible)

This is **enterprise grade**: deterministic rules + auditable AI extraction (AI doesn’t “decide”, it extracts structured facts).

---

## 1) User Types (who uses it)

### U1 Employer HR / People Ops
- Plans hiring route, manages candidate lifecycle, onboarding tasks, approvals.

### U2 Hiring Manager / Panel
- Interview kits, structured notes, decision room rationale.

### U3 Worker / Contractor / Employee
- Upload documents once, resolve blockers, submit timesheets, view payment status.

### U4 Finance / Payroll Ops
- Payroll readiness, pay approvals, exports, reconciliation, disputes.

### U5 IT / Security Admin
- Access readiness, control requirements, escalations, ticket exports.

### U6 Compliance / Admin
- Policy packs, country rules, audit logs, model/prompt configs, retention.

---

## 2) Why the pain exists (current reality + workaround)

### Where existing systems stop
- ATS: hires pipeline, not cross-border operational cutovers.
- HRIS: records employee, not dependency reasoning across borders.
- Payroll/EOR: executes payroll, but requires perfect inputs and chases exceptions.
- ITSM: tickets, not compliance readiness logic.
- Docs tools: storage, not validation + dependency propagation.

### Workaround today
Spreadsheets + email ping-pong + “tribal knowledge” across HR/Legal/IT/Finance.  
Time cost per cross-border hire: **10–30 hours** of coordination (distributed), plus delays and rework.

---

## 3) Scope Boundaries (explicit: what it does and doesn’t)

### It DOES
- Compute readiness & blockers from evidence + rules
- Build dependency task graphs
- Validate documents deterministically
- Manage timesheets + approvals
- Manage payroll pay-run readiness + exports + reconciliation
- Handle phase changes and recompute on events
- Maintain audit trail

### It does NOT
- Calculate taxes
- Provide immigration/visa advice as legal authority
- Replace payroll providers/EORs (it integrates)
- Make final hiring decisions automatically

---

## 4) Scenario Universe (not just 2)

### Reference Implementation Scenario (RS-1)
**Employee in Y, remote start from X (30 days), then relocate to Y.**  
This scenario must always work end-to-end including payroll/timesheet.

### Additional mandatory scenarios (must be supported)
S2 Contractor in X → convert to employee in Y  
S3 Employee in Y, remote forever from X  
S4 Worker relocates earlier/later than planned  
S5 Name mismatch blocks tax/bank acceptance  
S6 ID expires before pay run  
S7 Role changes requiring higher access tier mid-cycle  
S8 Vendor lead time longer than expected (start date at risk)  
S9 Multiple hires across 5+ countries concurrently (portfolio view + scaling)  
S10 Security incident forces immediate access downgrade (no payroll disruption)

---

## 5) Screens (exact count + where CV/LinkedIn/notes live + payroll/timesheets)

### Employer/HR app — 12 screens
1. **Dashboard** (portfolio overview: hires, blockers, deadlines, pay runs)
2. **Global Hiring Planner** (route planning + feasibility + phases)
3. **Role Setup** (rubric + requirements + interview rounds)
4. **Candidate Inbox** *(includes Import Sources panel: CV + LinkedIn + Portfolio)*
5. **Candidate Profile** *(includes Evidence Map tab: CV/LinkedIn/portfolio/notes)*
6. **Interview Kit** (agenda + notes capture + structuring)
7. **Decision Room** (panel + rationale + contradictions)
8. **Offer Builder** (terms + contract pack + approvals)
9. **Onboarding Orchestrator** (task graph across HR/Worker/IT/Finance)
10. **Access Readiness** (phase-based access set + controls)
11. **Timesheet & Work Hub** (work logs, timesheets, approvals)
12. **Payroll & Pay Run Hub** (pay-run readiness, exports, reconciliation)

### Worker app — 7 screens
1. **Worker Home** (status, phase, countdowns, blockers)
2. **Document Vault** (upload + validate + field view)
3. **Tasks & Requests** (task list + “what next”)
4. **Work Log** (daily/weekly work entries)
5. **Timesheet Submission** (hours + approvals + anomalies)
6. **Payments Status** (paid/pending/disputed)
7. **Notifications/Alerts** (drift, blockers, pay, access)

### Admin/Compliance — 5 screens
1. **Policy Pack Manager**
2. **Country Rules Library**
3. **Audit Log Viewer**
4. **Retention & Deletion Console**
5. **Model/Prompt Config**

**Total screens: 24.**  
This includes what you called out: **CV/LinkedIn/portfolio + interview notes + payroll/timesheet/work tracking**.

## 6) Tech Stack (explicit, not vague)

### Frontend
- Next.js + TypeScript
- Auth: Auth.js (or Clerk) — choose one in build
- API client: fetch + typed OpenAPI client

### Backend
- Python FastAPI
- Background jobs: Celery + Redis
- DB: PostgreSQL + pgvector
- Storage: S3/R2 (signed URLs)

### AI
- LLM for extraction/structuring: OpenAI (model chosen in config)
- Embeddings for search/evidence retrieval: OpenAI embeddings (model chosen in config)

### Observability
- OpenTelemetry
- Structured logs
- AI run logging (tokens/latency/model)

---

## 7) AI usage (% and why, no toy usage)

### AI is “heavy” (high usage) in these areas:
- **Doc extraction** (ID/address/tax/bank/contracts)
- **CV/LinkedIn/portfolio structuring** (facts extraction)
- **Interview notes structuring** (convert raw notes → evidence)
- **Evidence map building** (link claims ↔ evidence)
- **Task recommendation (“what next?”)**

### AI is NOT used for final compliance decisions
Final readiness is computed by deterministic rules using extracted facts.

### Approx AI usage share (by runtime calls, not “marketing”)
- Document & profile extraction: **45%**
- Evidence mapping + retrieval: **20%**
- Notes structuring + contradiction checks: **15%**
- Task recommendation + summaries: **10%**
- Drift scanning explanations (alert text): **10%**

So this is not “minimal AI”; it’s AI-first, but in safe, auditable lanes.

## 12) Deterministic Rules (where “truth” lives)

AI extracts facts; **rules decide**.

### Examples (non-exhaustive)
- ID validity: `expiry_date > now()`
- Address proof recency: `issue_date >= now()-90d`
- Payroll readiness: requires specific facts present by pay-run cutoff
- Access tier rules by phase/security level
- Name canonicalization rules (ID is canonical)
- Tax form completeness and signature presence

Rules are versioned in `rule_versions` and referenced in audit logs.

---

## 13) Payroll + Timesheet + Work Tracking (explicit, no shrink)

### Work Log (not optional in enterprise)
- Worker logs “work performed” (text + tags) daily/weekly.
- Used for:
  - evidence of work continuity
  - dispute support
  - anomaly explanation context

### Timesheet (operational)
- Worker submits hours
- AI flags anomalies (A21)
- HR/Manager approves
- Finance consumes

### Pay Run (enterprise)
- Pay run has:
  - period
  - cutoff date
  - planned pay date
  - readiness rules (must be met before export)
- Export to payroll vendor (stub integration)
- Reconcile paid vs expected (A22)
- Disputes logged with evidence references

**This is the missing piece you called out — now fully included.**

## 15) Testing (acceptance + failure tests per AI call)

### A) Unit tests (deterministic engine)
- rule_engine: every rule pass/fail
- phase_engine: phase transitions
- dependency_engine: DAG ordering, cycle detection
- payroll_engine: pay-run readiness gating
- timesheet_engine: approval and state transitions

### B) AI contract tests (per A-call)
For each A-call (A1..A32):
- **schema validation** (Pydantic)
- **idempotency test** (same input → same stored result ref)
- **failure handling** (AI error → task remains blocked, alert created)

### C) Scenario acceptance tests (end-to-end)
- RS-1 full run:
  - plan → candidate → offer → onboarding → remote phase → timesheets → pay run → relocation event → phase 2 → access switch → pay run
- Failures:
  - expired ID before pay date → blocks pay run + remediation task
  - name mismatch → blocks tax form acceptance
  - relocation delayed → recompute tasks + extend phase safely

### D) Load/performance assumptions (explicit)
- Document extraction: target p95 < 10s (async job acceptable)
- Recompute on event: < 3s for task graph + readiness evaluation (AI optional if only rule recompute)
- Pay run readiness: deterministic and fast (no AI needed)

## 17) Who pays / who uses / free vs paid (brutal)

### Likely paying customers (B2B)
- Companies doing cross-border hiring at scale
- EOR/payroll vendors (as an “exception reduction” layer)
- Staffing agencies

### Who expects free
- Workers (portal access free, employer pays)
- Small teams may use a limited tier

### Value drivers (time/money)
- HR coordination time reduced (10–30h → 3–8h per complex hire)
- Fewer missed start dates
- Fewer payroll rejections and payment disputes
- Lower IT rework and audit risk
